[00:48:25] receiver gets the first packet of a
[00:48:27] message, it knows exactly how much more

[00:48:33] for doing congestion control. Second,
[00:48:36] Home Prioritizes shorter messages. It
[00:48:39] uses SRPT, shortest remaining processing
[00:48:41] time first to try and prioritize shorter
[00:48:44] messages. And third, because messages
[00:48:48] are all independent, they're not

[00:48:53] is independent. Shorter messages can

[00:49:04] from the receiver. Now, when you think
[00:49:06] about it, this makes sense because the
[00:49:08] congestion happens primarily at that
[00:49:10] last down link to the receiver. And so,
[00:49:13] the receiver has way more information.
[00:49:15] In fact, with HOMA, as soon as it gets
[00:49:17] the first packet of a message, it knows
[00:49:19] exactly how much more is coming. So, it
[00:49:20] has essentially complete information
[00:49:23] about congestion and it can therefore
[00:49:26] respond to congestion much more quickly
[00:49:28] and much more precisely.
[00:49:31] The way things work with HOMA is that
[00:49:33] when a sender has a message to send, it
[00:49:35] breaks it up into packets, but it only
[00:49:38] transmits the first few packets, those
[00:49:40] are called unscheduled packets, to the
[00:49:42] receiver. Packets after that are called
[00:49:44] scheduled packets and they only get
[00:49:47] transmitted when the receiver asks for
[00:49:49] them. So the receiver will send grant
[00:49:51] packets back. It'll paste them out and
[00:49:54] send those back to the sender over time
[00:49:55] telling the sender it's now time for you
[00:49:57] to send me the next chunk of data. And
[00:50:00] the receiver can delay those grants. So
[00:50:02] for example, if the receiver has 10
[00:50:05] messages that are incoming, there's no
[00:50:07] point in sending grants to all 10 of
[00:50:08] them because then you'll just get
[00:50:09] congestion in the in the top of wrap
[00:50:12] cues. So it can use the grants to reduce
[00:50:15] congestion and then it can also use the
[00:50:17] grants to give preference to its most
[00:50:19] favorite messages which would be the
[00:50:21] shorter ones. So it's a way of of
[00:50:23] implementing SRPT by favoring short
[00:50:25] messages.
[00:50:28] The third aspect of Homa is that it
[00:50:30] takes advantage of the priority cues in
[00:50:32] modern switches. So modern data center
[00:50:34] switches have more than one Q at each
[00:50:36] egress port typically eight and they can
[00:50:39] be used in a priority mechanism where
[00:50:41] packets get transmitted preferentially
[00:50:43] from the highest priority Q. So I've

[00:50:47] typically there's more than that
[00:50:50] you can specify in packets using the
[00:50:52] various fields of the packet you can
[00:50:53] specify which queue it should go into
[00:50:55] and so Homa dynamically makes those
[00:50:57] choices in a way to give priority to
[00:51:00] shorter messages. So if we go back to
[00:51:02] the incast example from a few slides

[00:51:06] pile up in the lowest priority queue.

[00:51:16] cued packets from the short from the uh

[05:07:29] from Apache Flink which is a popular

[05:07:34] also from some of the exarchitects
[05:07:37] behind Meta Score event infra.
[05:07:41] So what are the ingredients in restate?
[05:07:43] Basically four parts. First of all, it
[05:07:46] makes sure that a single run of an agent
[05:07:48] is resilient. This is called durable
[05:07:51] execution in the industry. Think about
[05:07:54] things like when an agent runs for a
[05:07:56] week and then crashes. We want to be
[05:07:58] able to bring it back and let it
[05:08:00] continue exactly at the point where it
[05:08:02] failed. We don't want it to start over
[05:08:04] from the beginning.
[05:08:06] Another um area here is running many
[05:08:10] concurrent sessions in parallel. Imagine
[05:08:12] running thousands of concurrent agent
[05:08:14] sessions at the same time and needing
[05:08:16] needing to make sure that state is

[05:08:20] agents don't interfere with each other.

[05:08:25] communication between agents, between

[05:08:40] execution.
[05:08:43] So the way that you can think of it is

[05:21:49] Let's go back to the slides to have a
[05:21:52] little more of an idea of how this thing

[05:21:56] because it's actually a pretty
[05:21:58] interesting um design or architecture.
[05:22:03] So the way it's implemented is basically
[05:22:05] by having a a event-driven distributed
[05:22:08] log implementation.
[05:22:10] So inside the box you basically on one
[05:22:12] side have the clients on the other side
[05:22:14] the services and inside the box is a log
[05:22:18] which persists all those journal events
[05:22:20] and an event loop and that event loop
[05:22:22] basically gets the events from the
[05:22:24] service based on what the event is. It
[05:22:27] either persists some state in the
[05:22:29] embedded state store or it sets a timer
[05:22:32] or it sends a request to another agent.
[05:22:34] And by doing that you basically have a
[05:22:36] durable um foundation for whatever an
[05:22:40] application is doing.
[05:22:42] The design of this distributed log is
[05:22:45] heavily inspired by the way that the
[05:22:48] core event infra layer at meta works. Uh
[05:22:51] it's basically like an iteration on top
[05:22:53] of that. Um and some of those architects
[05:22:56] are now have designed that for restate
[05:22:59] as an more generic solution that is
[05:23:02] available in open source. There are two
[05:23:04] important things related to this
[05:23:06] architecture that make it interesting.
[05:23:08] The first one is that it works as a push
[05:23:10] model. So whereas most workflow
[05:23:12] orchestrators actually pull for new
[05:23:15] tasks um for pull from the workflow
[05:23:18] server, restate actually pushes the
[05:23:21] invocations and the benefit you get from
[05:23:23] that is that it has a much lower
[05:23:25] latency. So you can use these kind of
[05:23:28] workflow guarantees in functions around
[05:23:30] your application and uh have like a
[05:23:33] latency of for example 45 milliseconds
[05:23:36] p99 for like a 10-step workflow.
[05:23:40] Pushing invocations also works very well
[05:23:42] for serverless because they require you
[05:23:45] to basically uh send the request and
[05:23:48] wake up the function. So this design
[05:23:51] that I show here includes everything you
[05:23:54] need. It includes uh as well that state
[05:23:57] store where we were embedding the state
[05:23:59] as the UI. It's a single binary so it's
[05:24:02] pretty easy to operate as well to run it
[05:24:05] in like a highly available way. You just
[05:24:07] spin it up multiple times and let it

[06:48:24] can resume exactly where they were. And
[06:48:27] that's because the agent and its
[06:48:30] identity and history and its state is
[06:48:32] all captured in its data. The character
[06:48:35] lives in the data. And this is the
[06:48:37] framing that I want to bring to agents.
[06:48:41] Today when people talk about agents,
[06:48:43] they usually point at the wrong thing.
[06:48:46] They'll say that the agent is the model
[06:48:47] or they'll say that it's the runtime.
[06:48:49] And again, as I mentioned earlier, those
[06:48:51] things matter, but they're not the
[06:48:53] agent. The agent is its data. It's
[06:48:56] specifically the log. So what actually
[06:49:00] is the log? At the simplest level, the
[06:49:02] log is the appendon event history of the
[06:49:05] agent. It's every user input, every
[06:49:08] model output, every tool call, tool
[06:49:10] result, permission, failure. And the
[06:49:13] idea is that every state transition that
[06:49:16] the agent takes is written to the log.
[06:49:19] This is important because it means that
[06:49:22] the identity of the agent isn't tied to
[06:49:25] the runtime or the model or the tools.
[06:49:28] Those things are all just interpreting
[06:49:29] and appending to the log. They're
[06:49:31] reading the log, acting on it, and
[06:49:33] writing the next event back. And that's
[06:49:36] important because then just using the
[06:49:39] log on its own is enough to resume the
[06:49:43] agent. Once you define the agent as the
[06:49:45] log, the rest of the system becomes a
[06:49:47] whole lot easier to reason about because
[06:49:49] every operation is either reading from
[06:49:52] or appending to the log. The model is
[06:49:56] reading from the log and then
[06:49:58] determining the next action. The tool
[06:50:00] runner is then executing that action and
[06:50:02] then it's appending that result. And
[06:50:05] this is all operating in a loop.
[06:50:08] Everything coordinates itself around the
[06:50:10] log. In practice, a simplified loop can
[06:50:13] look something like this. You can
[06:50:14] reconstruct the state from the log. You
[06:50:16] can pass that state to the model. The
[06:50:18] model can propose the next step and then
[06:50:20] append that response to the log. If the
[06:50:22] response asks for a tool, you can run
[06:50:24] that tool and also append that response
[06:50:26] to the log and then you can repeat. The
[06:50:28] important insight is not that this loop
[06:50:30] is complicated. The important insight is
[06:50:33] that the loop is disposable. A worker
[06:50:35] can claim the session, read the log,
[06:50:37] advance the agent one step, write the
[06:50:39] result, and then just completely
[06:50:40] disappear. And then that means that any
[06:50:42] other worker can pick it up later.
[06:50:46] This pattern should feel familiar.
[06:50:48] Databases had to learn this first. For
[06:50:51] years, databases looked like these
[06:50:53] non-transparent systems that were hard
[06:50:55] to reason about with tables and indexes
[06:50:57] and materialized views. Underneath every
[06:51:00] serious database is a log and that log
[06:51:05] is the durable sequence of changes.
[06:51:08] Everything else is a view. I think
[06:51:11] agents need the same inversion. Today
[06:51:14] agents are treated as again these
[06:51:16] complicated systems that are opaque and
[06:51:18] they're filled with models and prompts
[06:51:20] and tool calls. But for the durable
[06:51:23] session, the log should be primary. The
[06:51:26] context that gets fed into the model is
[06:51:28] a projection of that log. The UI that
[06:51:30] gets rendered on top is a projection of
[06:51:32] that log. Debugging and traceability is
[06:51:35] a projection. Auditing is a projection.
[06:51:38] Compaction is also a projection which
[06:51:40] we'll talk about. But the log itself is
[06:51:43] not a projection. The log is the durable
[06:51:45] history that all of these projections
[06:51:47] can come from. Now there are two
[06:51:49] objections to the log as the agent that
[06:51:51] are worth discussing. So I'm going to
[06:51:53] talk about them now. Now let's start
[06:51:54] with compaction. A law can grow
[06:51:57] indefinitely, but a model's view of it
[06:52:00] can't. Context windows are finite. So
[06:52:03] eventually you do need to compact the
[06:52:05] log into a smaller representation that
[06:52:07] the model can reason about. But the
[06:52:10] important point is that this compaction
[06:52:11] is not magic and it doesn't break the
[06:52:14] claim that the log is the agent.
[06:52:16] Compaction is lossy. A compacted summary
[06:52:19] is not going to perfectly reproduce the
[06:52:22] state of the agent in a smaller form.
[06:52:24] It's actually going to throw information
[06:52:26] away. Point is the full log is the
[06:52:28] record and a compaction is just one
[06:52:31] projection of it. Just like how a
[06:52:33] materialized view is not the database or
[06:52:35] a summary of a conversation is not the
[06:52:37] conversation. If you keep the raw log,
[06:52:40] you can always generate new projections
[06:52:42] from it. But if you throw away the law
[06:52:44] log and keep only the compaction, you've
[06:52:47] effectively lost part of the agent. So
[06:52:49] it's cleanest to treat compaction as a
[06:52:52] best effort lossy fork, one that you can
[06:52:55] resume as a new log. The second
[06:52:58] objection is what about tools that
[06:53:00] change state outside of the log? And
[06:53:03] that's true. An agent can edit a file.
[06:53:05] It can create a GitHub issue. It can
[06:53:07] send an email. So clearly there is state
[06:53:09] outside of the log. But the point is is
[06:53:11] that the log is not supposed to contain
[06:53:14] the whole world. The log is just the
[06:53:16] agent's view of the world. It's just
[06:53:18] like how in the video game in Skyrim,
[06:53:21] the Skyrim save file doesn't contain the
[06:53:23] entire game engine or every asset in the
[06:53:25] map. It just contains the player
[06:53:27] specific state which is needed to drop
[06:53:29] you back into that world. And the same
[06:53:31] is true for the log and agents. The log
[06:53:35] can only faithfully resume or store that
[06:53:38] agent's identity and its view of the
[06:53:40] world, but it cannot make that world
[06:53:42] deterministic. If the agent sent an
[06:53:44] email, forking back won't unend it. If
[06:53:47] some file got changed underneath, the
[06:53:50] agent won't know about it. But the log's
[06:53:52] job is to record what the agent did,
[06:53:55] what it saw, what changed, and what it
[06:53:57] needs to continue. It stores that
[06:54:00] identity, and that's its purpose. And
[06:54:02] much like that Skyrim character save
[06:54:04] file, it's not meant to store the entire
[06:54:07] world. It's just meant to store its view
[06:54:10] of it. So once you start treating the
[06:54:12] log as a primitive, a whole bunch of
[06:54:14] system properties will fall out
[06:54:15] naturally. So the first property is
[06:54:18] reliability. Consider what happens today
[06:54:21] with cloud code. If you're using cloud
[06:54:23] code and your agent reaches a permission
[06:54:25] prompt and the process dies for whatever
[06:54:28] reason and then you resume it, the
[06:54:31] permission prompt will be gone and the
[06:54:33] agent will be paused and that is
[06:54:36] unacceptable in production. The
[06:54:38] permission prompt should stay there. So
[06:54:41] this is just a sign of when you
[06:54:43] architect your agent in a way where the
[06:54:46] log isn't the agent. when the log is the
[06:54:49] agent.
